The Harry Potter films had some pretty strange things happen during shoots, from vandalism to actor accidents to hilarious hijinks that Fred and George Weasley would definitely approve of. Hearing about all these antics might just make you wish you'd gotten that Hogwarts acceptance letter after all. So load up on chocolate frogs and head to Platform 9 3⁄4 so we can revisit some of the strangest things to happen on the set of Harry Potter. Trashing the Train The Hogwarts Express was the site of not one but two cases of property destruction during the shoots. In 2003, a group of youths made good on that Marauder's Map pledge to be up to no good and spray-painted one of the train cars with silver and green symbols while it was stationed at a depot in Scarborough. As if that wasn't bad enough, in 2007, another set of vandals smashed several windows with the train's emergency hammers, and the boys responsible, who were between 12 and 14 at the time, were arrested for causing such expensive damage to the vehicle. Hamster Funeral On the set of Harry Potter and the Sorcerer's Stone, personal tragedy struck young star Emma Watson, who played Hermione Granger in the film. Her pet hamster, Millie, died of a heart attack during filming, and since Watson was only 11 years old at the time, it was a devastating day for her indeed. The film's set department conjured up an idea for how to make her feel better about it, though, and made Watson's lost little pal her own tiny mahogany casket with velvet lining and a silver plaque bearing her name. Close call. Water scenes in movies are notoriously difficult to pull off, so it's probably no surprise that the scene where the young competitors in the Triwizard Tournament were made to fish out their friends from the depths of the sea became problematic. Daniel Radcliffe, who played Harry, got his signals crossed when he was filming the diving scene in a water tank, and, instead of using the gesture that meant A-OK, he accidentally alerted his scuba trainers that he was running out of air and needed to be brought to the surface. It was a relatively harmless snafu in the end, of course, but it's a good thing he didn't make the wrong sign at a time of need. Tragedy strikes. While Radcliffe's close call was a false alarm, there was a gruesome accident on the set of Harry Potter and the Deathly Hallows in 2009 that left Radcliffe's stunt double David Holmes permanently paralyzed. The 25-year-old former gymnast was filming a flying scene that required him to be yanked backward during a controlled explosion. Something went wrong and he was thrown to the ground. As a result, Holmes, who had been Radcliffe's stunt double since the first movie, suffered a broken neck and is still in a wheelchair. Despite this tragedy, Holmes has maintained his relationship with some of the Potter cast members, especially Radcliffe and Tom Felton, who play Draco Malfoy. He's also an official ambassador for the Royal National Orthopedic Hospital of Peel. where he did his strenuous recovery after the accident. Hogwarts Alight Holmes' devastating injury wasn't the only set accident to occur on Deathly Hallows. Due to a pyrotechnic special effect failure, a fire broke out during the Hogwarts battle scene, catching the actual set on fire. None of the main cast was present, but 100 crew members were there, and it took 40 minutes to put the fire out. Luckily, the castle that burned was a mocked-up version. In fact, no major damage was caused to the main set. and the fire was out by the time the fire department got there. Though tabloids exaggerated the incident, Warner Bros. representatives said the set was going to be rebuilt anyway for Deathly Hallows Part II. Even so, sources joked that the crew was forced to write, I must not burn down Hogwarts using the blood quill. Mischief managed. Class clown. During the filming of Sorcerer's Stone, one actor's performance was so hilarious that the kids on the set couldn't stop giggling. which was a problem in keeping to the production schedule. Peeves, the poltergeist of Hogwarts, was a staple throughout the books, and Rick Mayall was cast for the part. Problem was, he had the kids in stitches so much that he was even asked to do his lines with his back to the kids, and even then, they still couldn't stop laughing at him. It wasn't the incessant chuckling that got the character axed from the theatrical cut, though. Director Chris Columbus didn't like the way it looked on screen and left his scenes on the editing room floor, much to the chagrin of Mayall. Mayall later said that he didn't have the heart to tell his own children he'd been cut, so when they went to see the film, they came home and said, "'Bloody good makeup, Dad,' thinking that it was him who played Hagrid in the movie." Homework sessions Harry Potter and the Prisoner of Azkaban director Alfonso Cuarón forced the main actors to write essays about the characters they'd been playing for years. As the story goes, Radcliffe wrote one page, Watson wrote 16, and Rupert Grint pulled a classic Ronald Weasley move and just didn't turn his in. Cuaron claimed this exercise was a way to immerse the actors in their own emotions and experiences while digging deeper into the characters they play, and he said their essays were really beautiful, very honest, very bare, and very courageous. Sucker Punch In the scene from Prisoner of Azkaban where Hermione calls Draco Malfoy a foul, loathsome, evil little cockroach, she was supposed to just slap him, according to Tom Felton. But apparently, Watson got carried away and punched her co-star instead. Felton says she has a mean hook, and that he walked away from the incident with his tail tucked between his legs and his hearing sort of impaired. Let's relive that moment once again, shall we? Chances are, he won't be calling anyone a mudblood in real life anytime soon with the memory of that hit in mind. Thanks for watching. Click the grunge icon to subscribe to our YouTube channel. The Harry Potter world may seem like a wondrous place filled with magic, butterbeer, and chocolate frogs, but there are still some pretty dark and twisted parts to the stories. The series may have been kid-friendly at first, but it quickly did away with any age constraints to cast its spell on generations. thanks in part to some surprisingly grown-up elements. From suggestive names to infuriating character choices, here are some aspects of the Harry Potter series that take on a whole new meaning for adult audiences. Negligence or abuse Dumbledore does a great job of passing himself off as a kindly, benevolent father figure to Harry and the rest of the student body. But if you take a close look at his actual actions, He's a pretty icy person who knowingly sends children into harm's way for the sake of an obscure prophecy. And his willingness to throw Harry to the wolves, sometimes literally speaking, is just part of the series' troubling trend of adults being negligent or even possibly abusive to these kids. Hagrid regularly writes out roadmaps for the main trio to waltz into danger. I shouldn't have said that. I should not have said that. The faculty is filled with crazy people, and even the parents are pretty lousy. How else could you explain Hermione's folks, who are muggles, allowing their 11-year-old to run through a wall and hop on a train they never even saw? Or how no one bothered to check on baby Harry Potter for a decade after he was dropped off at the doorstep of an aunt and uncle everyone knew were terrible? Good adulting, everybody. The Ministry of what? There are tons of pseudo-political commentaries contained in the Harry Potter series. Gellert Grindelwald tries to enslave the muggle population by way of a global wizarding war that, not coincidentally, rages alongside the real-life events of World War II. Voldemort and his Death Eaters fight for genetic supremacy over half-bloods and muggles, like some of the worst villains in history. But perhaps the most unsettling aspect of the stories is that the Ministry of Magic remains in place without any checks or balances and seems to oppress certain classes of wizards. They have a totalitarian approach to justice, with boundary-free surveillance options at their disposal and an army of soul-sucking dementors who inflict cruel punishment on prisoners. They're also in charge of all the money and sometimes distribute assets unfairly between various families. It just goes to show that even at its best, the wizarding world is never truly free. Just ask the house elves. Fat-shaming It gets a laugh out of a lot of readers, but the way J.K. Rowling writes about Dudley Dursley in the earliest installments of the Harry Potter series is kind of troubling. Although she has plenty of faults to choose from while writing about Harry's spoiled cousin, she pays a lot of attention to the fact that he's portly, or, as she writes it, the size of a young killer whale. With descriptions of Dudley's piggly little eyes and his resemblance to a pig in a wig, Red is especially cruel and unnecessary in retrospect, even if Rowling has insisted she's against fat-shaming. Sexual innuendos The Harry Potter books may be meant for kids, but certain parts belong in the restricted section. For example, there's the Ingorgio spell, which seems like a poor choice of name on Rowling's part. and is probably the only explanation for how Hagrid's father made Whoopi with a giantess. I shouldn't have said that. I should not have said that. Then there's the fact that these are all teenage students who've had no sex ed coursework at all. Some have traced the timing of Nymphadora Tonk's conception to her parents' teen years. Rowling herself has said she didn't mean to encourage teen pregnancy in the story. But thanks to a dirty Easter egg in the Marauder's Map from the Prisoner of Azkaban movie, we know Hogwarts could get pretty steamy with or without proper education about safe sex. Wasted Gifts As creative and thorough as Rowling was when creating the Potterverse, there are some aspects of the stories that become pretty frustrating in hindsight. Snape has the gift of legitimacy, for example. but doesn't bother using it to figure out that Barty Crouch Jr. is drinking Polyjuice potion to disguise himself as Mad-Eye Moody in The Goblet of Fire. Seems like that might have been important. And Hermione's time turner, a literal time travel device, could have been used for far more important things than overloading her school schedule and springing an animal from execution. Harry could have probably used that luck potion he gets in Half-Blood Prince to help him defeat Voldemort. And Minerva McGonagall's shapeshifting skills should have given her an upper hand in undermining Dolores Umbridge, who just so happens to be a sucker for cats. And the consequences may be severe. Instead, all of these elements just come and go as trivial moments of convenience, despite the fact that there is an all-out war in motion. And let's not forget how casually Dumbledore gifts Harry one of the three sacred Deathly Hallows, the Cloak of Invisibility. long before he could come anywhere near appreciating its true value. Whoa! Whatever. Why were the Weasleys so poor? It's been common knowledge that the Weasley family weren't amongst the most wealthy of folk in the wizarding world. From hand-me-down robes and garments to second-hand books, the Weasley family really did squeeze every penny. Their house seemed to be one giant room, with small sub-rooms, and looked as if it was poorly constructed. However, it was a home, and it was their home. Now, there appear to be several reasons as to why the Weasleys had so little money. Molly Weasley in particular was a stay-at-home mother and therefore did not work, leaving Arthur as the sole provider for the family. She made the children clothes and knew some amazing food-related charms. Arthur, as a kind and decent man he was, really did lack ambition to succeed with his career. He had no desire for progression or promotion and watched for years as many others moved above him in rank and pay grade. He had become somewhat of a laughing stock according to some. None of his sons aspired to be like him, Percy in particular, who once even highlighted his disgust at how poor the family were. We've also got the fact that the Weasleys were quite a large family. As the family expanded, Arthur's wage became smaller. I believe they did manage up until the point of Bill and Charlie's Hogwarts days. and it was at this point that they simply could not afford the new books and uniforms as Percy, Fred and George followed suit within the next six years. Alex from ATM, and welcome to Behind the Screen, where we take a closer look at the real-life inspiration behind your favorite TV shows and movies. Today, I'm taking a look at possibly the UK's biggest franchise. Harry Potter. We all know that J.K. Rowling has taken a huge amount of inspiration from a billion other sources. So let's delve into the wizarding world and see if we can uncover some origin stories for some of the most famous characters in history. It's time to head Behind the Screen. Harry Potter. Let's start on the boy Wonder. While he might seem like an iconic hero, J.K. Rowling's protagonist draws significant inspiration from the ancient Greek hero Achilles. According to various myths, Achilles was the son of the sea nymph Thetis and King Peleus of the Myrmidons. In one version of the legend, just like Harry, a prophecy uttered that Thetis' child would be greater than its father. And both Achilles and Harry were gifted an extraordinary level of protection from their mothers. Lily Potter died to save Harry, casting the ancient Sacrifice Protection counter-charm which continued to protect Harry throughout his life. Similarly, Achilles was plunged into the River Styx by his mother, Thetis, to try and make him immortal. The only part of him that remained above the water was his left ankle, which is where she was holding him. Alongside their maternal protection, both Harry and Achilles were hidden away during their childhood. Achilles was purposefully hidden in the court of the King of Skyros to protect him from the Trojan War, and Harry was hidden in the Muggle world of the Dursleys. Hermione Granger. In Greek mythology, Hermione was the only child of King Menelaus of Sparta and his wife, Helen of Troy. As any fans of classics or the iconic 2004 movie Troy will know, Paris, Prince of Troy, fell in love with Helen while she was still married, and the two ran away to Troy to be together. After Hermione's mother abandoned her, her father Menelaus set off to reclaim Helen and started the Trojan War. While Hermione Granger didn't exactly get left by her parents, she did have to abandon them by wiping their memories of her while she was searching for Horcruxes. Plus, mythical Hermione was initially betrothed to Orestes, her first cousin twice removed, whatever that means. But then, her father also promised her to Neo-Ptolemus, Achilles' son. This whole situation can be seen echoed in the Ron slash Victor Crumb love triangle idea that appears in Goblet of Fire. Albus Dumbledore. Albus Dumbledore is often described as the most powerful wizard of his generation. It's clear that J.K. Rowling took significant inspiration from one of the most powerful figures in Arthurian legend, Merlin. Whilst numerous sources debate what actually happened to Merlin, some argue that he serviced King Arthur as an advisor before Merlin was imprisoned by the Lady of the Lake. which bears an eerie resemblance to this scene from Half-Blood Prince. In one poetic telling of Merlin, he is described as a man with a preternatural knowledge of the past, present and future, as given to him by both a demonic entity and, weirdly, God. Hey, sometimes it's good to work together. This slightly echoes Dumbledore's life and the loss of his moral compass when he fell in love with Grindelwald and considered the idea of wizarding dominion over muggles. Fenrir Greyback. From Arthurian legend to Norse mythology. Fenrir Greyback is a werewolf noted for his horrendous savagery, particularly towards children, and his desire to spread lycanthropy to as many people as possible. Fenrir is named after the monstrous wolf of Norse mythology. Fenrir is the son of Loki and the sibling of Hel, the goddess of the underworld and the serpent Jormungandr. All three children were prophesied to cause great mischief and disaster. Fenrir in particular is pretty important, because he is prophesized to kill Odin during Ragnarok, the end of the world, but will in turn be killed by Odin's son, Vithar. Sirius Black. The name Sirius comes from the brightest star in the night sky, and is more commonly known as the Dog Star because of its constellation of Calus Major, or Greater Dog. In Greek mythology, Sirius is the deity of the dog star and has appeared in various guises, including Mayra, the dog of Icarus, Leilaps, the dog of Orion, and Chion Chryseos, the golden hound of Zeus. Sirius Black's animagus of a black hound has even wider significance. In British folklore, the black dog is also associated with the devil and death. Remus Lupin. Remus Lupin is named after the myth of twin brothers Remus and Romulus from Roman mythology. Remus and Romulus were born to Rhea Silva and the god Mars. Their mother was a vestal virgin and the daughter of a displaced king. The twins were a threat to the new king who then ordered their deaths, and to save them, Rhea Silvia abandoned them by the river, but they were rescued by the god Tibernius. The twins were suckled by a she-wolf in a cave and were later adopted by a shepherd, Faustulus. It was only when the twins discovered their true identities did they then decide to found the city of Rome, but they disagreed on the location. In anger, Romulus killed his brother and went on to found Rome and serve as its first king. While Romulus doesn't appear in the Harry Potter world, unless I'm mistaken, if I am, let me know in the comments, it is Remus Lupin's codename on Potterwatch. Minerva McGonagall. The head of Gryffindor House is based on and named after the Roman goddess of wisdom and war, Minerva. Alongside Juno and Jupiter, Minerva serves as one of the three supreme leaders of Roman mythology. She is also goddess of medicine, the arts, poetry, and handicrafts. And interestingly enough, Minerva's symbol is also the owl. Sibyl Trelawney. Arguably the most erratic of the professors at Hogwarts was the ever-strange Professor Trelawney, who was deceptive. Uploading the big video. ...the son of the river god Cephasus and Liriope the nymph. He was a hunter renowned for his beauty, but also, troublingly, his pride. Ovid tells the story of Narcissus and Echo, a mountain nymph who fell in love with Narcissus and tried to embrace him. He rejected her and she was so heartbroken she spent the rest of her life alone in the glens, fading away, until just an Echo of her remained. Incidentally, this is where we get the term Echo from. Narcissus instantly fell in love with the image and stared at it until he lost all will to live and died, still looking at himself. Draco Malfoy In the Malfoy family, all names have some relevance, and Draco is no different. Draco is Latin for dragon and is also the name of one of the 88 constellations in the night sky. In Roman mythology, Draco was a dragon killed by the goddess Minerva, who threw his body into the sky. But as she threw it, it became twisted and froze at the North Pole before it could right itself. You're not still on about that bloody dragon, are you? Interestingly, Draco is sometimes represented as the son of Gaea, the personification of Earth, and Typhon, a monstrous snake-like giant who was considered the deadliest creature in Greek mythology. Argus Filch. But it isn't all pureblood wizards who get the luxury of a mythological origin. Hogwarts' eternally grumpy caretaker, Argus Filch, is based on Argus Panotes. a giant from Greek mythology with 100 eyes. Argus was the servant of Hera, the Greek goddess of marriage, women, childbirth and family, and crucially, the wife of Zeus. Argus was tasked by Hera to guard a white cow, who was actually Io, a nymph that Zeus desired. To rescue Io, Zeus had Hermes, the messenger god, murder Argus by blinding him and then hitting him with a rock. According to legend, Hera then had Argus' 100 eyes preserved forever in the peacock's tail. And finally, Alastor Mad-Eye Moody. Alastor was actually an epithet of Zeus, which described him as the avenger of evil thieves, particularly with regards to familial bloodshed. The name Alastor was used to describe the personification of a curse or any deity or demon who avenged the wrongs committed by men. Correct! Which makes it the perfect name for an aura who tracks down and enacts justice on dark witches or wizards. But did you know that many of the quotes in the books and movies actually had some spoilers and clues for future events hidden inside them? Here are some examples that were casually said in the series and are overlooked, but through rereading and reviewing the films, they secretly reveal things to come. [00:33:13.350 -> 00:33:23.850]  The Harry Potter films had some pretty strange things happen during shoots, from vandalism to actor accidents to hilarious hijinks that Fred and George Weasley would definitely approve of.
[00:33:24.080 -> 00:33:53.060]  Hearing about all these antics might just make you wish you'd gotten that Hogwarts acceptance letter after all. Trashing the Train The Hogwarts Express was the site of not one but two cases of property destruction during the shoots. In 2003, a group of youths made good on that Marauder's Map pledge to be up to no good and spray-painted one of the train cars with silver and green symbols while it was stationed at a depot in Scarborough.
[00:33:53.410 -> 00:34:20.550]  As if that wasn't bad enough, in 2007, another set of vandals smashed several windows with the train's emergency hammers, and the boys responsible, who were between 12 and 14 at the time, were arrested for causing such expensive damage to the vehicle. Hamster Funeral On the set of Harry Potter and the Sorcerer's Stone, personal tragedy struck young star Emma Watson, who played Hermione Granger in the film. Her pet hamster, Millie, died of a heart attack during filming, and since Watson was only 11 years old at the time,
[00:34:20.770 -> 00:34:51.120]  it was a devastating day for her indeed. The film's set department conjured up an idea for how to make her feel better about it, though, and made Watson's lost little pal her own tiny mahogany casket with velvet lining and a silver plaque bearing her name. Close call. Water scenes in movies are notoriously difficult to pull off, so it's probably no surprise that the scene where the young competitors in the Triwizard Tournament were made to fish out their friends from the depths of the sea became problematic. Daniel Radcliffe, who played Harry,
[00:34:51.450 -> 00:35:20.550]  got his signals crossed when he was filming the diving scene in a water tank, and, instead of using the gesture that meant A-OK, he accidentally alerted his scuba trainers that he was running out of air and needed to be brought to the surface. It was a relatively harmless snafu in the end, of course, but it's a good thing he didn't make the wrong sign at a time of need. Tragedy strikes. While Radcliffe's close call was a false alarm, there was a gruesome accident on the set of Harry Potter and the Deathly Hallows in 2009 that left Radcliffe's stunt double David Holmes permanently paralyzed.
[00:35:21.080 -> 00:35:49.120]  The 25-year-old former gymnast was filming a flying scene that required him to be yanked backward during a controlled explosion. Something went wrong and he was thrown to the ground. As a result, Holmes, who had been Radcliffe's stunt double since the first movie, suffered a broken neck and is still in a wheelchair. Despite this tragedy, Holmes has maintained his relationship with some of the Potter cast members, especially Radcliffe and Tom Felton, who play Draco Malfoy. He's also an official ambassador for the Royal National Orthopedic Hospital of Peel.
[00:35:49.400 -> 00:36:16.400]  where he did his strenuous recovery after the accident. Hogwarts Alight Holmes' devastating injury wasn't the only set accident to occur on Deathly Hallows. Due to a pyrotechnic special effect failure, a fire broke out during the Hogwarts battle scene, catching the actual set on fire. None of the main cast was present, but 100 crew members were there, and it took 40 minutes to put the fire out. Luckily, the castle that burned was a mocked-up version. In fact, no major damage was caused to the main set.
[00:36:16.670 -> 00:36:41.090]  and the fire was out by the time the fire department got there. Though tabloids exaggerated the incident, Warner Bros. representatives said the set was going to be rebuilt anyway for Deathly Hallows Part II. Even so, sources joked that the crew was forced to write, I must not burn down Hogwarts using the blood quill. Mischief managed. Class clown. During the filming of Sorcerer's Stone, one actor's performance was so hilarious that the kids on the set couldn't stop giggling.
[00:36:41.340 -> 00:37:01.260]  which was a problem in keeping to the production schedule. Peeves, the poltergeist of Hogwarts, was a staple throughout the books, and Rick Mayall was cast for the part. Problem was, he had the kids in stitches so much that he was even asked to do his lines with his back to the kids, and even then, they still couldn't stop laughing at him. It wasn't the incessant chuckling that got the character axed from the theatrical cut, though.
[00:37:01.480 -> 00:37:29.620]  Director Chris Columbus didn't like the way it looked on screen and left his scenes on the editing room floor, much to the chagrin of Mayall. The Harry Potter world may seem like a wondrous place filled with magic, butterbeer, and chocolate frogs, but there are still some pretty dark and twisted parts to the stories. The series may have been kid-friendly at first, but it quickly did away with any age constraints to cast its spell on generations.
[00:53:06.160 -> 00:53:29.960]  thanks in part to some surprisingly grown-up elements. From suggestive names to infuriating character choices, here are some aspects of the Harry Potter series that take on a whole new meaning for adult audiences. Negligence or abuse Dumbledore does a great job of passing himself off as a kindly, benevolent father figure to Harry and the rest of the student body. But if you take a close look at his actual actions,
[00:53:30.450 -> 00:53:52.530]  He's a pretty icy person who knowingly sends children into harm's way for the sake of an obscure prophecy. And his willingness to throw Harry to the wolves, sometimes literally speaking, is just part of the series' troubling trend of adults being negligent or even possibly abusive to these kids. Hagrid regularly writes out roadmaps for the main trio to waltz into danger.
[00:53:53.710 -> 00:54:21.610]  I shouldn't have said that. I should not have said that. The faculty is filled with crazy people, and even the parents are pretty lousy. How else could you explain Hermione's folks, who are muggles, allowing their 11-year-old to run through a wall and hop on a train they never even saw? The Ministry of what?
[00:54:22.180 -> 00:54:44.800]  There are tons of pseudo-political commentaries contained in the Harry Potter series. Gellert Grindelwald tries to enslave the muggle population by way of a global wizarding war that, not coincidentally, rages alongside the real-life events of World War II. Voldemort and his Death Eaters fight for genetic supremacy over half-bloods and muggles, like some of the worst villains in history.
[00:54:45.250 -> 00:55:12.990]  But perhaps the most unsettling aspect of the stories is that the Ministry of Magic remains in place without any checks or balances and seems to oppress certain classes of wizards. They have a totalitarian approach to justice, with boundary-free surveillance options at their disposal and an army of soul-sucking dementors who inflict cruel punishment on prisoners. They're also in charge of all the money and sometimes distribute assets unfairly between various families.
[00:55:13.470 -> 00:55:42.830]  It just goes to show that even at its best, the wizarding world is never truly free. Just ask the house elves. Fat-shaming It gets a laugh out of a lot of readers, but the way J.K. Rowling writes about Dudley Dursley in the earliest installments of the Harry Potter series is kind of troubling. [00:55:43.260 -> 00:56:08.640]  With descriptions of Dudley's piggly little eyes and his resemblance to a pig in a wig, Red is especially cruel and unnecessary in retrospect, even if Rowling has insisted she's against fat-shaming. Alex from ATM, and welcome to Behind the Screen, where we take a closer look at the real-life inspiration behind your favorite TV shows and movies. Today, I'm taking a look at possibly the UK's biggest franchise. Harry Potter. We all know that J.K. Rowling has taken a huge amount of inspiration from a billion other sources. So let's delve into the wizarding world and see if we can uncover some origin stories for some of the most famous characters in history. It's time to head Behind the Screen.
[05:36:04.890 -> 05:36:33.650]  Harry Potter. Let's start on the boy Wonder. While he might seem like an iconic hero, J.K. Rowling's protagonist draws significant inspiration from the ancient Greek hero Achilles. According to various myths, Achilles was the son of the sea nymph Thetis and King Peleus of the Myrmidons. In one version of the legend, just like Harry, a prophecy uttered that Thetis' child would be greater than its father. And both Achilles and Harry were gifted an extraordinary level of protection from their mothers.
[05:36:34.340 -> 05:36:57.940]  Lily Potter died to save Harry, casting the ancient Sacrifice Protection counter-charm which continued to protect Harry throughout his life. Similarly, Achilles was plunged into the River Styx by his mother, Thetis, to try and make him immortal. The only part of him that remained above the water was his left ankle, which is where she was holding him. Alongside their maternal protection, both Harry and Achilles were hidden away during their childhood.
[05:36:58.500 -> 05:37:26.350]  Achilles was purposefully hidden in the court of the King of Skyros to protect him from the Trojan War, and Harry was hidden in the Muggle world of the Dursleys. Hermione Granger. In Greek mythology, Hermione was the only child of King Menelaus of Sparta and his wife, Helen of Troy. As any fans of classics or the iconic 2004 movie Troy will know, Paris, Prince of Troy, fell in love with Helen while she was still married, and the two ran away to Troy to be together.
[05:37:26.860 -> 05:37:52.000]  After Hermione's mother abandoned her, her father Menelaus set off to reclaim Helen and started the Trojan War. While Hermione Granger didn't exactly get left by her parents, she did have to abandon them by wiping their memories of her while she was searching for Horcruxes. Plus, mythical Hermione was initially betrothed to Orestes, her first cousin twice removed, whatever that means. But then, her father also promised her to Neo-Ptolemus, Achilles' son.
[05:37:52.450 -> 05:38:22.000]  This whole situation can be seen echoed in the Ron slash Victor Crumb love triangle idea that appears in Goblet of Fire. Albus Dumbledore. Albus Dumbledore is often described as the most powerful wizard of his generation. It's clear that J.K. Rowling took significant inspiration from one of the most powerful figures in Arthurian legend, Merlin. Whilst numerous sources debate what actually happened to Merlin, some argue that he serviced King Arthur as an advisor before Merlin was imprisoned by the Lady of the Lake.
[05:38:22.330 -> 05:38:51.830]  which bears an eerie resemblance to this scene from Half-Blood Prince. In one poetic telling of Merlin, he is described as a man with a preternatural knowledge of the past, present and future, as given to him by both a demonic entity and, weirdly, God. Hey, sometimes it's good to work together. This slightly echoes Dumbledore's life and the loss of his moral compass when he fell in love with Grindelwald and considered the idea of wizarding dominion over muggles. Fenrir Greyback. From Arthurian legend to Norse mythology.
[05:38:52.480 -> 05:39:15.940]  Fenrir Greyback is a werewolf noted for his horrendous savagery, particularly towards children, and his desire to spread lycanthropy to as many people as possible. Fenrir is named after the monstrous wolf of Norse mythology. Fenrir is the son of Loki and the sibling of Hel, the goddess of the underworld and the serpent Jormungandr. All three children were prophesied to cause great mischief and disaster.
[05:39:16.700 -> 05:39:39.420]  Fenrir in particular is pretty important, because he is prophesized to kill Odin during Ragnarok, the end of the world, but will in turn be killed by Odin's son, Vithar. Sirius Black. The name Sirius comes from the brightest star in the night sky, and is more commonly known as the Dog Star because of its constellation of Calus Major, or Greater Dog.
[05:39:39.980 -> 05:40:08.870]  In Greek mythology, Sirius is the deity of the dog star and has appeared in various guises, including Mayra, the dog of Icarus, Leilaps, the dog of Orion, and Chion Chryseos, the golden hound of Zeus. Sirius Black's animagus of a black hound has even wider significance. In British folklore, the black dog is also associated with the devil and death. Remus Lupin. Remus Lupin is named after the myth of twin brothers Remus and Romulus from Roman mythology.
[05:40:09.400 -> 05:40:31.960]  Remus and Romulus were born to Rhea Silva and the god Mars. Their mother was a vestal virgin and the daughter of a displaced king. The twins were a threat to the new king who then ordered their deaths, and to save them, Rhea Silvia abandoned them by the river, but they were rescued by the god Tibernius. The twins were suckled by a she-wolf in a cave and were later adopted by a shepherd, Faustulus.
[05:40:32.670 -> 05:41:04.060]  It was only when the twins discovered their true identities did they then decide to found the city of Rome, but they disagreed on the location. In anger, Romulus killed his brother and went on to found Rome and serve as its first king.
